Overview

SkyLite is a server-centric .NET framework for IIS and .NET Framework 4.8, built for rapid development of data-driven business applications in VB.NET or C#.

SkyLite and SkyNet. SkyLite runs on the .NET Framework 4.8 under IIS on Windows. SkyNet is its successor — the same server-driven model rebuilt on ASP.NET Core / .NET 10, so it runs on Windows, Linux and macOS. The page model, the ApiResponse command set and the client engine carry over almost unchanged; this guide describes SkyLite.

What's the SkyLite ?

  SkyLite is a server-centric .NET framework designed for the rapid development of modern, data-driven web applications. It empowers developers to build rich user interfaces and complex server-side logic primarily using VB.NET or C#, significantly reducing the need for extensive client-side JavaScript. The framework is particularly suited for creating robust business applications, such as ERPs, CRMs, and internal administrative tools, where data integrity, server-side control, and development speed are paramount. By abstracting away much of the boilerplate HTML and complex AJAX communication, SkyLite allows developers to focus on core application functionality while maintaining a clear and maintainable codebase.

Architectural Philosophy

  The core architecture of the SkyLite Framework revolves around the WebPage base class and a well-defined request lifecycle. Each page in an application is represented by a class that inherits from WebPage, which automatically handles incoming GET and POST requests. During an initial GET request, the framework follows a distinct lifecycle, including the crucial OnInitialized method where developers programmatically construct the user interface using a rich set of UI controls. This server-driven approach ensures that business logic and UI generation are tightly integrated and managed within a robust, type-safe .NET environment, providing a single source of truth for the application's state and presentation.

UI Development and Controls

  UI development in SkyLite is streamlined through a comprehensive set of server-side UI controls available in the skylite.ToolKit namespace. Controls such as Texts, Dropdown, Grid, Button, and various layout containers (HtmlElementBox, Wrap) abstract away the underlying HTML markup. This allows developers to define the interface declaratively in their preferred .NET language. Each control exposes objects, properties, and methods for granular control over attributes, styles, and event handlers. The final HTML for a control is generated by calling its .HtmlText() method, which can then be combined and rendered to the client. This methodology promotes code reusability and simplifies the creation of complex, data-bound forms and displays.

Client-Server Communication Model

  A defining feature of the SkyLite Framework is its simplified model for asynchronous client-server communication, which operates without traditional page refreshes. This interaction is primarily managed by two core components: the client-side $ApiRequest JavaScript function and the server-side ApiResponse class. User actions in the browser (e.g., a button click) call the $ApiRequest function, which seamlessly sends data to a corresponding public function on the server's WebPage class. The server-side function processes the request and returns an ApiResponse object. This object acts as a command container, instructing the client's core JavaScript library to perform actions such as navigating to a new page (.Navigate()), displaying a popup (.PopUpWindow()), or dynamically updating a specific element's content on the page (.SetElementContents()). This powerful pattern gives developers full server-side control over real-time client-side updates.

Getting Started

What you need installed, how to mount a SkyLite application in IIS, and where to get the framework.

Getting Started with the SkyLite Framework

  Welcome to the SkyLite Framework! SkyLite is a .NET framework designed for the rapid development of modern, data-driven web applications, particularly for business contexts like ERP, CRM, and internal tools. It leverages the power of server-side VB.NET or C# to control the UI, while using a lightweight, built-in JavaScript library to handle client-server communication without full page reloads.

SkyLite AI Code Assistant

The SkyLite AI Code Assistant represents a powerful and specialized implementation of Google's advanced AI, meticulously trained and prompted to serve as an expert co-pilot for the SkyLite web framework. This isn't a general-purpose chatbot; it's a fine-tuned development tool designed to understand the unique architecture, controls, and best practices of SkyLite, transforming natural language requests into high-quality, ready-to-use code.

Current Functionality (Within Google AI Studio):

At its core, the SkyLite AI Code Assistant leverages a sophisticated, context-rich prompt that encapsulates the entirety of the SkyLite framework's documentation and real-world code examples. This allows it to function as an interactive "code generator" with the following key capabilities:

  • Requirement-to-Code Translation: The assistant excels at taking high-level, descriptive user requirements (e.g., "create a login page with an email field, a password field, and a submit button") and translating them into a complete set of files (.vb/.cs, .js, .css).
  • Framework-Specific Expertise: Unlike generic coding assistants, this tool understands and correctly utilizes SkyLite's proprietary UI controls (Texts, SQLGridSection, HtmlElementBox, etc.), its unique AJAX communication model ($ApiRequest and ApiResponse), and its server-centric lifecycle (OnInitialized, WebPage inheritance).
  • Architectural Pattern Adherence: The assistant has been trained on the "Main View" / "Edit View" (MV/EV) pattern and the use of a custom WebBase class. It consistently generates code that is not only functional but also architecturally sound and maintainable within the SkyLite ecosystem.
  • Complete Solution Generation: It doesn't just provide isolated code snippets. For any given task, it generates all necessary components: the server-side logic in VB.NET or C#, the corresponding client-side JavaScript for event handling, and the CSS for styling, ensuring a fully integrated and working solution.

Future Vision: From Prompt to Productized AI Agent

The current implementation in Google AI Studio is the foundational "brain" of a much larger vision. The future of the SkyLite AI Code Assistant is to evolve from an interactive prompt into a fully-fledged, standalone AI Agent that can be seamlessly integrated into a developer's workflow. This future includes:

  • Integration with a Host Application: The next step is to build a "host" application (which could be a SkyLite web app itself) that connects the AI's "brain" (the Gemini API powered by this prompt) to a developer's local file system.
  • Autonomous File System Operations: The agent will be given "tools" to perform real-world actions. Instead of just displaying code, it will be able to Create and Write Files(.vb, .js, .css), Modify Existing Code, Scaffold Entire Modules.
  • Architectural Pattern Adherence: The assistant has been trained on the "Main View" / "Edit View" (MV/EV) pattern and the use of a custom WebBase class. It consistently generates code that is not only functional but also architecturally sound and maintainable within the SkyLite ecosystem.
  • Marketplace Potential: As you've envisioned, this standalone agent becomes a highly valuable, distributable product. The ultimate future is an "App Store for AI Agents," where specialized tools like the SkyLite AI Code Assistant can be discovered and used by developers worldwide. This creates a new ecosystem where expert knowledge (like your framework design) can be packaged and monetized, empowering a new generation of developers to build powerful applications faster than ever before.

In summary, the SkyLite AI Code Assistant, in its current form, is a powerful proof-of-concept. Its future lies in becoming an autonomous agent that bridges the gap between conversational instruction and tangible, production-ready code, dramatically accelerating the entire software development lifecycle for the SkyLite framework.

Prerequisites

  Before you begin, ensure you have the following installed and configured:

  • Microsoft Visual Studio (2013 or later)
  • .NET Framework (4.5 or later)
  • Internet Information Services (IIS) or IIS Express installed on your development machine.

How to Install IIS on Windows 10 and Windows 11 (Skip this when using IIS Express)

These instructions apply to all modern versions of Windows, including Pro and Enterprise editions.

Step 1: Open "Turn Windows features on or off"

This is the central location for adding optional Windows components like IIS. You can get here in two common ways:

Method A: Using the Control Panel

  1. Open the Control Panel.
  2. Go to Programs.
  3. Under Programs and Features, click on Turn Windows features on or off.

Method B: Using Windows Search (Faster)

  1. Click the Start Menu or press the Windows key.
  2. Type "Turn Windows features on or off" and press Enter.

Step 2: Select the IIS Features

A dialog box will appear with a list of features. You need to enable not just the basic IIS, but also the specific components required for ASP.NET applications like SkyLite. Expand the trees and check the boxes to exactly match the provided image.

  1. Find and expand Internet Information Services.
  2. Expand World Wide Web Services.
  3. Under Application Development Features, select:
    • .NET Extensibility 4.8 (or the highest 4.x version shown)
    • ASP.NET 4.8 (or the highest 4.x version shown)
    • CGI
    • ISAPI Extensions
    • ISAPI Filters
    • WebSocket Protocol
  4. Under Common HTTP Features, select:
    • Default Document
    • Directory Browsing
    • HTTP Errors
    • Static Content
  5. Under Health and Diagnostics, select:
    • HTTP Logging
  6. Under Performance Features, select:
    • Static Content Compression
  7. Under Security, select:
    • Basic Authentication
    • Request Filtering
  8. Go back to the top level and expand Web Management Tools.
    • Ensure that IIS Management Console is checked. This is required to manage your websites.

Step 3: Install the Features

  1. Click OK.
  2. Windows will find the required files and install the selected features. This process may take a few minutes.
  3. Once completed, Windows will notify you. It might require a system restart. If it does, restart your computer.

Step 4: Verify the Installation

To confirm that IIS was installed correctly:

  1. Open any web browser (Edge, Chrome, Firefox).
  2. Navigate to the address: http://localhost
  3. If the installation was successful, you will see the default blue IIS Welcome Page.

Step 5: Ensure ASP.NET is Registered with IIS (If Necessary)

If you installed Visual Studio before installing IIS and you run into issues, you may need to ensure ASP.NET is properly registered.

  1. Open Command Prompt or PowerShell as an Administrator.
  2. Navigate to the .NET Framework directory: cd C:\Windows\Microsoft.NET\Framework64\v4.0.30319
  3. Run the registration command: aspnet_regiis.exe -i

You have now successfully installed and configured IIS with the specific features required for SkyLite development.

Hello, World

Four worked pages, from a single greeting to a fuller form, each showing the same four-file page anatomy.

Hello, World

  This guide will walk you through setting up your environment and creating your first simple, interactive "Hello, World" page.

Step 1: Project Setup

  1. Create an ASP.NET Project: In Visual Studio, create a new ASP.NET Empty Web Application. Name your project (e.g., "MySkyLiteApp").
  2. Create Folder Structure: In the Solution Explorer, create the following folders in the root of your project:
    • App_Code (for your .vb or .cs page classes)
    • appConfig (to hold application.cfg, configuration text file)
    • bin (to hold the SkyLite DLL)
    • data (for your data)
    • htmls (for your html files)
    • images (for your image files)
    • logs (system error log files)
    • scripts (for your .js files)
    • styles (for your .css files)
    • temp (for your .vb or .cs page classes)
  3. Mount in IIS: Open IIS Manager and mount your project folder as a new application under the "Default Web Site". This allows you to access your pages via a URL like http://localhost/MySkyLiteApp/.

Step 2: Framework Integration

  1. Add the DLL: Place the skylite.dll (or skycore.dll) file into the "bin" folder of your project.
  2. Configure web.config: This is the most critical step. Create a web.config file in the root of your project (or modify the existing one) and add the SkyLite HTTP handler. This handler intercepts all incoming requests and routes them through the framework.
    3. Web.config
    <?xml version="1.0" encoding="utf-8"?>
<configuration>
  <system.webServer>
    <handlers>
      <!-- This handler directs all requests to the SkyLite framework -->
      <add name="SkyLite" verb="*" path="*" type="skylite.IHandler" />
    </handlers>
  </system.webServer>
</configuration>

Step 3: Creating Your First Page ("MyPage")

We will create a simple page that takes a user's name as input and displays a greeting when a button is clicked. This requires four files.

  1. Client-Side HTML (MyPage.html): Create a new HTML file named MyPage.html in the "htmls" folder. This file contains the initial UI structure.
    2. <h3>Simple Greeting Application</h3>

<div id="greeting-message" style="margin-top:10px; font-weight:bold;">
    <!-- The server will place the greeting here -->
</div>

<hr />

<p>Please enter your name:</p>
<input type="text" id="nameInput" />
<button onclick="SayHello()">Say Hello</button>
  2. Client-Side JavaScript (MyPage.js): Create a new JavaScript file named MyPage.js inside the scripts folder.
    3. function SayHello() {
    // Get the value from the input box.
    var name = document.getElementById('nameInput').value;

    // Package the data to send to the server.
    var data = [
        { key: 'userName', vlu: name }
    ];

    // Call the server-side function with the same name ('SayHello').
    // The framework automatically finds the matching function in the MyPage class.
    $ApiRequest(this, JSON.stringify(data));
}
  3. Server-Side Code (Choose VB.NET or C#): Create a new Class file named MyPage inside the App_Code folder.
    4. VB
    Imports skylite
Imports skylite.ToolKit

Public Class MyPage
    Inherits WebPage

    ' This function is called by the $ApiRequest in MyPage.js
    Public Function SayHello() As ApiResponse
        ' Get the 'userName' value sent from the client.
        Dim userName As String = GetDataValue("userName")

        ' Create the greeting message.
        Dim greeting As String = "Hello, " & userName & "!"

        ' Create a response object.
        Dim _ApiResponse As New ApiResponse()

        ' Command the client to update the content of the 'greeting-message' div.
        _ApiResponse.SetElementContents("greeting-message", greeting)

        ' Send the command back to the client.
        Return _ApiResponse
    End Function
End Class
    C#
    using skylite;
using skylite.ToolKit;

public class MyPage : WebPage
{
    // This function is called by the $ApiRequest in MyPage.js
    public ApiResponse SayHello()
    {
        // Get the 'userName' value sent from the client.
        string userName = GetDataValue("userName");

        // Create the greeting message.
        string greeting = $"Hello, {userName}!";

        // Create a response object.
        var _ApiResponse = new ApiResponse();

        // Command the client to update the content of the 'greeting-message' div.
        _ApiResponse.SetElementContents("greeting-message", greeting);

        // Send the command back to the client.
        return _ApiResponse;
    }
}
  4. Stylesheet (MyPage.css): (Optional) Create a new CSS file named MyPage.css inside the styles folder.
    5. button {
    padding: 5px 10px;
    cursor: pointer;
}

#greeting-message {
    color: blue;
}

Step 4: Understanding the Workflow

The SkyLite framework automatically connects these files based on their names.

  1. Initial Page Load (GET Request):
    • You navigate to http://localhost/MySkyLiteApp/MyPage.
    • The skylite.IHandler in web.config intercepts the request.
    • It finds the MyPage class and the MyPage.html file.
    • It executes the OnLoad lifecycle event, automatically loading the content of MyPage.html into the HtmlDoc.HtmlBodyText property.
    • It then automatically links MyPage.js and MyPage.css.
    • The final, complete HTML document is rendered and sent to your browser.
  2. User Interaction (POST Request):
    • You type a name and click the "Say Hello" button.
    • The onclick="SayHello()" event fires the JavaScript function in MyPage.js.
    • The $ApiRequest() function sends the input data to the server.
    • The skylite.IHandler again intercepts the request. Because it's a POST from $ApiRequest, it knows to look for a public function.
    • It finds the Public Function SayHello() in the MyPage class.
    • The function runs, gets the data with GetDataValue, and builds an ApiResponse.
    • The ApiResponse is sent back to the browser as a JSON object containing the command (SetElementContents) and its parameters ("greeting-message", "Hello, [Name]!").
    • The built-in SkyLite JavaScript on the client processes this command, updating the div in real-time.

Step 5: Running the Application

Save all files. Open your web browser and navigate to the URL you configured in IIS (e.g., http://localhost/MySkyLiteApp/MyPage). You should see the input field and button. Type your name, click the button, and the greeting message will appear without the page reloading.

Hello, World3

  This guide will walk you through creating simple "Hello, World" page with Toolkit's UI controls.

Creating Your First Page ("MyPage")

  1. Client-Side HTML (MyPage.html): Create a new HTML file named MyPage.html in the "htmls" folder. This file contains the initial UI structure.
    2. There is no separate .html file; the UI is built entirely on the server.
  2. Client-Side JavaScript (MyPage.js): Create a new JavaScript file named MyPage.js inside the scripts folder.
    3. This example is for display and layout purposes only and does not require JavaScript.
  3. Server-Side Code (Choose VB.NET or C#): Create a new Class file named MyPage inside the App_Code folder.
    4. VB
    Imports skylite
Imports skylite.ToolKit

Public Class MyPage
    Inherits WebPage

    Public Overrides Sub OnInitialized()
        Dim Hello As New HtmlTag(HtmlTags.h3)
        Hello.InnerText = "Hello World3"

        HtmlDoc.HtmlBodyText = Hello.HtmlText
    End Function

End Class
    C#
    using skylite;
using skylite.ToolKit;

public class MyPage : WebPage
{
    public override void OnInitialized()
    {
        HtmlTag Hello = new HtmlTag(HtmlTags.h3);
        Hello.InnerText = "Hello World3";

	HtmlDoc.HtmlBodyText = Hello.HtmlText();
    }
}

Hello, World4

  This guide will walk you through creating simple "Hello, World" page with both html file and Toolkit's UI controls.

Creating Your First Page ("MyPage")

  1. Client-Side HTML (MyPage.html): Create a new HTML file named MyPage.html in the "htmls" folder. This file contains the initial UI structure.
    2. <h3>Hello {name}</h3>
  2. Client-Side JavaScript (MyPage.js): Create a new JavaScript file named MyPage.js inside the scripts folder.
    3. This example is for display and layout purposes only and does not require JavaScript.
  3. Server-Side Code (Choose VB.NET or C#): Create a new Class file named MyPage inside the App_Code folder.
    4. VB
    Imports skylite
Imports skylite.ToolKit

Public Class MyPage
    Inherits WebPage

    Public Overrides Sub OnInitialized()

        ' Read Html file and replace "{name}" with "World4"
	Dim HelloHtmlText As String = ReadHtmlFile("MyPage").Replace("{name}", "World4")

        ' It displays in the browser.
	HtmlDoc.HtmlBodyText = HelloHtmlText

    End Function
End Class
    C#
    using skylite;
using skylite.ToolKit;

public class MyPage : WebPage
{
    public override void OnInitialized()
    {

        // Read Html file and replace "{name}" with "World4"
	string HelloHtmlText = ReadHtmlFile("MyPage").replace("{name}", "World4");

        // It displays in the browser.
	HtmlDoc.HtmlBodyText = HelloHtmlText;

    }
}

AJAX Technique

How SkyLite replaces hand-written AJAX with a single call out and a list of DOM commands back.

AJAX Technique

(Asynchronous JavaScript and XML,Hybrid)

The Communication Bridge: $ApiRequest and ApiResponse

  The cornerstone of the SkyLite Framework's dynamic, single-page-like behavior is its asynchronous communication model. This model operates without requiring full page reloads and is orchestrated by two primary components: the client-side JavaScript function $ApiRequest and the server-side .NET class ApiResponse. Together, they form a powerful bridge that allows server-side code to seamlessly control and update the user's browser in real-time.

The Client-Side Initiator: $ApiRequest

  The $ApiRequest is a built-in JavaScript function provided by the SkyLite core library that initiates all asynchronous communication from the client to the server. Its primary role is to abstract away the complexities of underlying AJAX (Asynchronous JavaScript and XML) calls, providing developers with a simple and consistent method for invoking server-side logic.

  Main Features:

  • Simplified AJAX: Eliminates the need for developers to write manual XMLHttpRequest or fetch API code, reducing boilerplate and potential for errors.
  • Flexible Data Transmission: Easily packages and sends client-side data (such as form inputs) to the server as a JSON-formatted string.
  • Implicit and Explicit Function Mapping:
    • By default ($ApiRequest(this, ...)), it invokes a server-side function that has the same name as the calling JavaScript function.
    • It also allows for explicitly targeting a different server-side function by name ($ApiRequest('ServerFunctionName', ...)), providing greater architectural flexibility.
  • Seamless Integration: It is designed to be called directly from client-side event handlers (e.g., onclick, onchange) defined in either SkyLite UI controls or pure HTML.

The Server-Side Command Center: ApiResponse

  The ApiResponse is a server-side .NET class that serves as the response mechanism for any function called via $ApiRequest. Rather than simply returning data, an ApiResponse object functions as a "command container." Developers add a series of commands to this object, which are then serialized and sent to the client. The SkyLite core JavaScript library interprets these commands and executes them in sequence, allowing for precise and powerful manipulation of the web page from the server.

  Main Features:

  • Real-time DOM Manipulation: Provides a rich set of methods to dynamically update the live HTML Document Object Model (DOM) without a page refresh. This includes changing an element's content (SetElementContents), replacing an entire element (ReplaceElement), and modifying attributes (SetElementAttribute) or styles (SetElementStyle).
  • User Interaction and Feedback: Includes simple methods to provide immediate feedback to the user, such as displaying a modal dialog box (PopUpWindow) or a simple alert (MessageBox).
  • Server-Controlled Navigation: Allows server-side logic to dictate page flow by instructing the browser to navigate to a new page (Navigate, Navigate2) or open a new window (NewWindow).
  • Client-Side Data Management: Offers methods to programmatically manage client-side storage from the server, including setting and removing cookies (SetCookie, RemoveCookie) and interacting with Local/Session Storage.
  • Custom Script Execution: Provides the ability to execute arbitrary JavaScript code on the client (ExecuteScript), enabling advanced or highly custom client-side interactions to be triggered from the server.

$ApiRequest

The client-side entry point: how a page method is invoked and what travels with the request.

$ApiRequest

  The $ApiRequest function is the primary client-side mechanism for initiating asynchronous POST requests to the server within the SkyLite framework. It encapsulates the underlying AJAX communication, providing a streamlined interface for sending data and handling the results of the server-side operation. Its asynchronous nature means it does not block the user interface while waiting for the server's response.

Syntax:
$ApiRequest(targetFunction, data, successCallback, errorCallback);
Parameters:
  • targetFunction (String | this) - Required, only when using with data. Specifies the server-side function to invoke.
    • If set to this, the framework will call the server-side function that has the same name as the currently executing JavaScript function. This is the most common usage.
    • If a String is provided (e.g., 'ProcessLogin'), the framework will explicitly call the server-side function with that specific name.
    • If there is no payload, it can be used as $ApiRequest();
  • data (JSON String) - Optional.. The payload to be sent to the server. This must be a JSON-stringified array of key-value objects in the format [{ key: 'keyName', vlu: 'value' }]. For requests that do not need to send data, an empty string '' can be used.

  • successCallback (Function) - Optional. A JavaScript function that will be executed after the server responds with a success status (HTTP 200) and all commands within the returned ApiResponse have been processed by the client. This is useful for performing client-side follow-up actions, such as re-enabling a button or clearing a form, after the server-side work is complete.

  • errorCallback (Function) - Optional. A JavaScript function that will be executed if the request fails due to a network error or if the server returns an error status (e.g., HTTP 500). This callback is ideal for handling unexpected issues, logging errors, or displaying a generic error message to the user.
Usage and Example:

  The primary use of $ApiRequest is to trigger server-side logic from a user action. The optional callback parameters are particularly powerful for managing the UI state during the asynchronous operation.

Consider a scenario where a "Save" button needs to be disabled during the request and re-enabled upon completion.

JavaScript (myPage.js): 
function saveProfile() {
    // Get the button and disable it to prevent multiple clicks
    var saveButton = document.getElementById('saveBtn');
    saveButton.disabled = true;

    // Collect data from the form
    var userName = document.getElementById('userName').value;
    var data = [
        { key: 'name', vlu: userName }
    ];

    // Define success and error callback functions
    var onSaveSuccess = function() {
        console.log("Server responded successfully.");
        saveButton.disabled = false; // Re-enable the button
    };

    var onSaveError = function() {
        alert("An error occurred while saving. Please try again.");
        saveButton.disabled = false; // Re-enable the button even on error
    };

    // Make the API call with all four parameters
    $ApiRequest('UpdateProfile', JSON.stringify(data), onSaveSuccess, onSaveError);
}
Server-Side (myPage.vb): 
Public Function UpdateProfile() As ApiResponse
    Dim userName As String = GetDataValue("name")

    ' --- Perform database update or other logic here ---

    ' Create a response to send back to the client
    Dim _ApiResponse As New ApiResponse
    _ApiResponse.PopUpWindow("Profile for " & userName & " has been updated.")
    Return _ApiResponse
End Function
Server-Side (myPage.cs): 
public ApiResponse UpdateProfile()
{
    string userName = GetDataValue("name");

    // --- Perform database update or other logic here ---

    // Create a response to send back to the client
    ApiResponse _ApiResponse = new ApiResponse();
    _ApiResponse.PopUpWindow("Profile for " + userName + " has been updated.");
    return _ApiResponse;
}

ApiResponse

The server-side reply object. Every method here maps to one command the client applies to the DOM.

ApiResponse

  The ApiResponse class is the central component for server-side control over the client's browser in the SkyLite framework. It serves as the required return type for any server-side function invoked by the client-side $ApiRequest function. Instead of returning raw data, ApiResponse acts as a powerful command container or instruction set. A developer instantiates an ApiResponse object, populates it with a sequence of commands by calling its methods, and then returns the object. The framework serializes these commands and sends them to the client, where SkyLite's core JavaScript library interprets and executes them in order, enabling dynamic and real-time manipulation of the web page without a full postback or page refresh.

Key Capabilities of ApiResponse

The methods of the ApiResponse class can be grouped into several logical categories:

  • DOM Manipulation: This is the most common use case. These methods allow for direct manipulation of HTML elements on the page, identified by their id, name, or a custom data-id attribute.
    • Examples: SetElementContents(), ReplaceElement(), SetElementAttribute(), SetElementStyle(), AddElementContents(), RemoveElement().
  • User Interaction & Feedback: These methods provide ways to communicate directly with the user.
    • Examples: PopUpWindow() (displays a modal with custom HTML content), MessageBox() (displays a simple browser alert), PopOff() (closes the most recent popup).
  • Navigation and Window Control: These methods control the browser's window and location.
    • Examples: Navigate() (redirects to a new page), Navigate2() (redirects with parameters), NewWindow() (opens a new browser window).
  • Client-Side Storage Management: These methods allow the server to manage data stored on the client's machine.
    • Examples: SetCookie(), RemoveCookie(), StoreLocalValue() (for localStorage), StoreSessionValue() (for sessionStorage).
  • Advanced Scripting and Server Calls: For more complex scenarios, these methods provide powerful control.
    • Examples: ExecuteScript() (runs a custom string of JavaScript on the client), ServerMethod() (triggers another server-side function from the response), CoreAction() (invokes a specific SkyLite server-side function).
Syntax & Usage Pattern:

The pattern for using ApiResponse is consistent across all server-side event handlers.

1. Instantiate: An object of the ApiResponse class is created.
2. Populate: One or more methods are called on the object, each representing a specific command to be executed on the client.
3. Return: The populated object is returned from the function to client browser.
C# Example: 
public ApiResponse ProcessForm()
{
    // 1. Instantiate
    ApiResponse response = new ApiResponse();

    // 2. Populate with commands
    response.SetElementContents("statusMessage", "Processing complete!");
    response.SetElementStyle("submitButton", "display", "none");
    response.Navigate("ConfirmationPage");

    // 3. Return
    return response;
}
VB Example: 
Public Function ProcessForm() As ApiResponse
    ' 1. Instantiate
    Dim response As New ApiResponse()

    ' 2. Populate with commands
    response.SetElementContents("statusMessage", "Processing complete!")
    response.SetElementStyle("submitButton", "display", "none")
    response.Navigate("ConfirmationPage")

    ' 3. Return
    Return response
End Function

SetBodyContents Method

  The ApiResponse.SetBodyContents method is a powerful command within the ApiResponse class that completely replaces the entire content of the <body> tag in the current HTML document. This action provides a way to perform a full-page update without a traditional browser navigation or refresh, offering a smoother user experience akin to a single-page application (SPA). It is particularly useful in scenarios where a user action results in a complete change of the view or context, such as switching between major sections of a dashboard or displaying the results of a complex search.

Syntax:
VB
' Overload 1: Using a String
Public Sub SetBodyContents(contentsHtml As String)

' Overload 2: Using an HtmlDocument object
Public Sub SetBodyContents(HtmlDoc As HtmlDocument)
C#
// Overload 1: Using a String
public void SetBodyContents(string contentsHtml)

// Overload 2: Using an HtmlDocument object
public void SetBodyContents(HtmlDocument HtmlDoc)
Parameters:
  • contentsHtml (String): A string containing the complete and well-formed HTML that will become the new inner HTML of the body tag.
  • HtmlDoc (HtmlDocument): A SkyLite HtmlDocument object. The framework will use the content of this object's HtmlBodyText property as the new content for the body tag. This overload is ideal when the new body content is programmatically constructed using SkyLite UI controls.
Usage and Example:

This method is invoked on an ApiResponse object within a server-side function. When the response is received by the client, the SkyLite core JavaScript library will execute this command, wiping the existing body content and inserting the new content.

Consider a dashboard page with a "Load Report" button. Clicking this button should replace the dashboard view with a report view, without the browser's refresh flicker. When LoadReport() is called, the entire content of the user's page body will be replaced by the sales report view, providing a seamless transition.

Server-Side (Dashboard.vb):
Public Function LoadReport() As ApiResponse
    ' Method 1: Building a simple HTML string
    Dim reportHtml As String = "<h1>Sales Report</h1><p>Report data goes here...</p>"

    ' Method 2 (Preferred): Using SkyLite controls to build the new body
    ' Assume GetReportView() is a function that returns a fully constructed
    ' HtmlDocument object containing the report UI.
    Dim reportDoc As HtmlDocument = GetReportView() ' This is a custom function

    ' Instantiate the response
    Dim _ApiResponse As New ApiResponse()

    ' Use the HtmlDocument overload to replace the body
    _ApiResponse.SetBodyContents(reportDoc)
    ' Or, for the simple string: _ApiResponse.SetBodyContents(reportHtml)

    Return _ApiResponse
End Function

Private Function GetReportView() As HtmlDocument
    Dim doc As New HtmlDocument()
    Dim reportGrid As New Grid() ' Create a grid for the report
    ' ... populate the grid ...
    doc.HtmlBodyText = "<h1>Sales Report</h1>" & reportGrid.HtmlText()
    Return doc
End Function
Server-Side (Dashboard.cs):
public ApiResponse LoadReport()
{
    // Method 1: Building a simple HTML string
    string reportHtml = "<h1>Sales Report</h1><p>Report data goes here...</p>";

    // Method 2 (Preferred): Using SkyLite controls to build the new body
    // Assume GetReportView() is a function that returns a fully constructed
    // HtmlDocument object containing the report UI.
    HtmlDocument reportDoc = GetReportView(); // This is a custom function

    // Instantiate the response
    ApiResponse _ApiResponse = new ApiResponse();

    // Use the HtmlDocument overload to replace the body
    _ApiResponse.SetBodyContents(reportDoc);
    // Or, for the simple string: _ApiResponse.SetBodyContents(reportHtml);

    return _ApiResponse;
}

private HtmlDocument GetReportView()
{
    HtmlDocument doc = new HtmlDocument();
    Grid reportGrid = new Grid(); // Create a grid for the report
    // ... populate the grid ...
    doc.HtmlBodyText = "<h1>Sales Report</h1>" + reportGrid.HtmlText();
    return doc;
}

AddBodyContents Method

  The ApiResponse.AddBodyContents method is a command within the ApiResponse class used to append HTML content to the end of the <body> tag of the current document. Unlike SetBodyContents, which replaces the entire body, AddBodyContents preserves the existing content and simply adds new content after it. This method is particularly useful for dynamically loading additional sections, views, or large data sets onto a page without affecting the already-rendered content. A common use case is implementing an "infinite scroll" feature, where new items are appended to the page as the user scrolls down.

Syntax:
VB
' Overload 1: Using a String
Public Sub AddBodyContents(contentsHtml As String)

' Overload 2: Using an HtmlDocument object
Public Sub AddBodyContents(HtmlDoc As HtmlDocument)
C#
// Overload 1: Using a String
public void AddBodyContents(string contentsHtml)

// Overload 2: Using an HtmlDocument object
public void AddBodyContents(HtmlDocument HtmlDoc)
Parameters:
  • contentsHtml (String): A string containing the complete and well-formed HTML that will become the new inner HTML of the body tag.
  • HtmlDoc (HtmlDocument): A SkyLite HtmlDocument object. The framework will use the content of this object's HtmlBodyText property as the new content for the body tag. This overload is ideal when the new body content is programmatically constructed using SkyLite UI controls.
Usage and Example:

This method is called on an ApiResponse object within a server-side function. When the response is processed by the client, the SkyLite core JavaScript library appends the specified HTML to the document body.

Consider a blog page that loads an initial set of posts and has a "Load More" button. Clicking this button should fetch and display the next set of posts at the bottom of the page.
When LoadMorePosts() is called, the new post "div" elements are added to the end of the page, seamlessly extending the content list for the user.

Server-Side (Blog.vb):
Imports skylite
Imports skylite.ToolKit

Public Class Blog
    Inherits WebPage

    ' This function is called by a "Load More" button on the client side.
    Public Function LoadMorePosts() As ApiResponse
        ' Get the current page number sent from the client's JavaScript.
        Dim currentPage As Integer = Integer.Parse(GetDataValue("currentPage"))
        Dim nextPage As Integer = currentPage + 1

        ' Assume GetPostsView() is a helper function that fetches posts for a given page
        ' and returns a constructed HtmlDocument object containing the new post elements.
        Dim nextPostsDoc As HtmlDocument = GetPostsView(nextPage)

        ' 1. Instantiate the response object.
        Dim _ApiResponse As New ApiResponse()

        ' 2. Use the HtmlDocument overload to append the new posts to the body.
        _ApiResponse.AddBodyContents(nextPostsDoc)
        
        ' 3. (Optional but good practice) Update the "Load More" button's onclick attribute
        '    to request the *next* page when clicked again.
        _ApiResponse.SetElementAttribute("loadMoreBtn", "onclick", $"LoadMorePosts({nextPage})")

        ' 4. Return the response object.
        Return _ApiResponse
    End Function

    ' A helper function to simulate fetching and formatting blog posts.
    Private Function GetPostsView(pageNumber As Integer) As HtmlDocument
        Dim doc As New HtmlDocument()

        ' --- Database logic to get posts for the specified 'pageNumber' would go here ---

        ' For each post retrieved, you would create a UI control (e.g., a Wrap or ContentsBox)
        ' and append its HtmlText() to doc.HtmlBodyText.

        ' This is a simplified example of the output.
        doc.HtmlBodyText = $"<div class='post'><h2>Post from Page {pageNumber}</h2><p>This is the content...</p></div>"

        Return doc
    End Function

End Class
Server-Side (Blog.cs):
using skylite;
using skylite.ToolKit;
using System;

public class Blog : WebPage
{
    // This function is called by a "Load More" button on the client side.
    public ApiResponse LoadMorePosts()
    {
        // Get the current page number sent from the client's JavaScript.
        int currentPage = Int32.Parse(GetDataValue("currentPage"));
        int nextPage = currentPage + 1;

        // Assume GetPostsView() is a helper function that fetches posts for a given page
        // and returns a constructed HtmlDocument object containing the new post elements.
        HtmlDocument nextPostsDoc = GetPostsView(nextPage);

        // 1. Instantiate the response object.
        ApiResponse _ApiResponse = new ApiResponse();

        // 2. Use the HtmlDocument overload to append the new posts to the body.
        _ApiResponse.AddBodyContents(nextPostsDoc);

        // 3. (Optional but good practice) Update the "Load More" button's onclick attribute
        //    to request the *next* page when clicked again.
        _ApiResponse.SetElementAttribute("loadMoreBtn", "onclick", $"LoadMorePosts({nextPage})");

        // 4. Return the response object.
        return _ApiResponse;
    }

    // A helper function to simulate fetching and formatting blog posts.
    private HtmlDocument GetPostsView(int pageNumber)
    {
        HtmlDocument doc = new HtmlDocument();

        // --- Database logic to get posts for the specified 'pageNumber' would go here ---

        // For each post retrieved, you would create a UI control (e.g., a Wrap or ContentsBox)
        // and append its HtmlText() to doc.HtmlBodyText.

        // This is a simplified example of the output.
        doc.HtmlBodyText = $"<div class='post'><h2>Post from Page {pageNumber}</h2><p>This is the content...</p></div>";
        
        return doc;
    }
}

SetElementAttribute Method

  The ApiResponse.SetElementAttribute method is a fundamental command used to dynamically add or modify an HTML attribute of a single, specific element on the client's page. This action is performed in real-time without a page refresh. It identifies the target element using its unique id attribute. This method is essential for altering the state, behavior, or appearance of an element based on server-side logic. Common use cases include disabling a button after it has been clicked, changing the src of an image, updating the href of a link, or applying a new CSS class to an element.

Syntax:
VB
Public Sub SetElementAttribute(elementId As String, attribute As String, value As String)
C#
public void SetElementAttribute(string elementId, string attribute, string value)
Parameters:
  • elementId (String): Required. The unique id of the HTML element you want to modify.
  • attribute (String): Required. The name of the HTML attribute to set or change (e.g., "disabled", "class", "src", "placeholder", "data-custom-value"). For convenience and to avoid typos, you can use the constants provided in the HtmlAttributes structure (e.g., HtmlAttributes.disabled)
  • value (String): Required. The new value to assign to the attribute. For boolean attributes like disabled or readonly, this value is typically set to "true" or the name of the attribute itself.
Usage and Example:

This method is a core part of managing UI state from the server. A classic example is disabling a button to prevent multiple submissions while a process is running on the server.

Client-Side HTML (or generated by OnInitialized):
<button id="processBtn" onclick="processData()">Process Data</button>
<div id="statusMessage"></div>
JavaScript (myPage.js):
function processData() {
    // No data needs to be sent for this example
    $ApiRequest('ProcessData');
}
VB.NET Server-Side Example (myPage.vb)
Imports skylite
Imports System.Threading

Public Class myPage
    Inherits WebPage

    Public Function ProcessData() As ApiResponse
        ' 1. Instantiate the response.
        Dim _ApiResponse As New ApiResponse()

        ' 2. Immediately disable the button on the client side.
        _ApiResponse.SetElementAttribute("processBtn", HtmlAttributes.disabled, "true")
        _ApiResponse.SetElementContents("statusMessage", "Processing... please wait.")

        ' --- Simulate a long-running server process ---
        Thread.Sleep(2000) ' Pauses for 2 seconds

        ' 3. After the process is complete, update the status and re-enable the button.
        _ApiResponse.SetElementContents("statusMessage", "Process completed successfully!")
        
        ' To re-enable, we simply remove the 'disabled' attribute.
        _ApiResponse.RemoveElementAttribute("processBtn", HtmlAttributes.disabled)

        ' 4. Return the response with all the commands.
        Return _ApiResponse
    End Function

End Class
C# Server-Side Example (myPage.cs)
using skylite;
using System.Threading;

public class myPage : WebPage
{
    public ApiResponse ProcessData()
    {
        // 1. Instantiate the response.
        ApiResponse _ApiResponse = new ApiResponse();

        // 2. Immediately disable the button on the client side.
        _ApiResponse.SetElementAttribute("processBtn", HtmlAttributes.disabled, "true");
        _ApiResponse.SetElementContents("statusMessage", "Processing... please wait.");

        // --- Simulate a long-running server process ---
        Thread.Sleep(2000); // Pauses for 2 seconds

        // 3. After the process is complete, update the status and re-enable the button.
        _ApiResponse.SetElementContents("statusMessage", "Process completed successfully!");
        
        // To re-enable, we simply remove the 'disabled' attribute.
        _ApiResponse.RemoveElementAttribute("processBtn", HtmlAttributes.disabled);

        // 4. Return the response with all the commands.
        return _ApiResponse;
    }
}

SetElementAttributeByName Method

  The ApiResponse.SetElementAttributeByName method is a command used to dynamically add or modify an HTML attribute for all elements on the client's page that share the same name attribute. This provides a powerful way to perform bulk updates on a group of related elements, such as a set of radio buttons or checkboxes, in a single command. Unlike SetElementAttribute which targets a unique id, this method offers a one-to-many relationship for attribute manipulation. It is particularly effective for tasks like checking or unchecking all items in a list, or applying a consistent data attribute to a group of inputs.

Syntax:
VB
Public Sub SetElementAttributeByName(elementName As String, attribute As String, value As String)
C#
public void SetElementAttributeByName(string elementName, string attribute, string value)
Parameters:
  • elementName (String): Required. The value of the name attribute shared by the group of HTML elements you want to modify.
  • attribute (String): Required. The name of the HTML attribute to set or change on each element in the group (e.g., "checked", "disabled", "class"). You can use the constants from the HtmlAttributes structure for consistency.
  • value (String): Required. The new value to assign to the attribute for every matched element.
Usage and Example:

This method is ideal for managing groups of form elements. A common use case is implementing "Select All" / "Deselect All" functionality for a list of checkboxes.

Client-Side HTML (or generated by OnInitialized):
<input type="checkbox" name="permission" value="read"> Read Access<br>
<input type="checkbox" name="permission" value="write"> Write Access<br>
<input type="checkbox" name="permission" value="delete"> Delete Access<br>
<br>
<button onclick="selectAll()">Select All</button>
<button onclick="deselectAll()">Deselect All</button>
JavaScript (myPage.js):
function selectAll() {
    // Call the server function to handle the "Select All" logic
    $ApiRequest('SelectAllPermissions', '');
}

function deselectAll() {
    // Call the server function to handle the "Deselect All" logic
    $ApiRequest('DeselectAllPermissions', '');
}
VB.NET Server-Side Example (myPage.vb)
Imports skylite

Public Class myPage
    Inherits WebPage

    Public Function SelectAllPermissions() As ApiResponse
        ' 1. Instantiate the response.
        Dim _ApiResponse As New ApiResponse()

        ' 2. Target all elements with name="permission" and set their "checked" attribute to "checked".
        _ApiResponse.SetElementAttributeByName("permission", HtmlAttributes.checked, "checked")

        ' 3. Return the response.
        Return _ApiResponse
    End Function

    Public Function DeselectAllPermissions() As ApiResponse
        ' 1. Instantiate the response.
        Dim _ApiResponse As New ApiResponse()

        ' 2. Target all elements with name="permission" and remove their "checked" attribute.
        _ApiResponse.RemoveElementAttributeByName("permission", HtmlAttributes.checked)

        ' 3. Return the response.
        Return _ApiResponse
    End Function

End Class
C# Server-Side Example (myPage.cs)
using skylite;

public class myPage : WebPage
{
    public ApiResponse SelectAllPermissions()
    {
        // 1. Instantiate the response.
        ApiResponse _ApiResponse = new ApiResponse();

        // 2. Target all elements with name="permission" and set their "checked" attribute to "checked".
        _ApiResponse.SetElementAttributeByName("permission", HtmlAttributes.checked, "checked");

        // 3. Return the response.
        return _ApiResponse;
    }

    public ApiResponse DeselectAllPermissions()
    {
        // 1. Instantiate the response.
        ApiResponse _ApiResponse = new ApiResponse();

        // 2. Target all elements with name="permission" and remove their "checked" attribute.
        _ApiResponse.RemoveElementAttributeByName("permission", HtmlAttributes.checked);

        // 3. Return the response.
        return _ApiResponse;
    }
}

RemoveElementAttribute Method

  The ApiResponse.RemoveElementAttribute method is a command used to dynamically remove an existing HTML attribute from a single, specific element on the client's page. This action is performed in real-time without a page refresh and identifies the target element using its unique id. Removing an attribute is the direct counterpart to setting one and is crucial for toggling states or behaviors that are controlled by the presence or absence of an attribute. The most common use cases are re-enabling a disabled element by removing the disabled attribute, making a field editable by removing the readonly attribute, or removing a class to revert an element's styling.

Syntax:
VB
Public Sub RemoveElementAttribute(elementId As String, attribute As String)
C#
public void RemoveElementAttribute(string elementId, string attribute)
Parameters:
  • elementName (String): Required. The unique id of the HTML element from which the attribute will be removed.
  • attribute (String): Required. The name of the HTML attribute to remove (e.g., "disabled", "class", "readonly"). For consistency and to prevent errors, it is recommended to use the constants provided in the HtmlAttributes structure (e.g., HtmlAttributes.disabled).
Usage and Example:

This method is frequently used in conjunction with SetElementAttribute to manage the state of UI elements. An excellent example is a form with an "Edit" button that, when clicked, makes several fields editable.

Client-Side HTML (or generated by OnInitialized):
<input type="text" id="username" value="JohnDoe" readonly="readonly">
<input type="text" id="email" value="johndoe@example.com" readonly="readonly">
<br>
<button id="editBtn" onclick="enableEditing()">Edit</button>
<button id="saveBtn" onclick="saveChanges()" style="display:none;">Save</button>
JavaScript (myPage.js):
function enableEditing() {
    // Call the server function to handle the UI changes
    $ApiRequest('EnableEditing');
}
function saveChanges() {
    // Logic to save data would go here
    $ApiRequest('SaveChanges');
}
VB.NET Server-Side Example (myPage.vb)
Imports skylite

Public Class myPage
    Inherits WebPage

    Public Function EnableEditing() As ApiResponse
        ' 1. Instantiate the response.
        Dim _ApiResponse As New ApiResponse()

        ' 2. Remove the "readonly" attribute from the input fields to make them editable.
        _ApiResponse.RemoveElementAttribute("username", HtmlAttributes.readonly)
        _ApiResponse.RemoveElementAttribute("email", HtmlAttributes.readonly)

        ' 3. Hide the "Edit" button and show the "Save" button.
        _ApiResponse.SetElementStyle("editBtn", "display", "none")
        _ApiResponse.SetElementStyle("saveBtn", "display", "inline-block")

        ' 4. Return the response.
        Return _ApiResponse
    End Function

    ' Placeholder for the SaveChanges function
    Public Function SaveChanges() As ApiResponse
        Return New ApiResponse()
    End Function

End Class
C# Server-Side Example (myPage.cs)
using skylite;

public class myPage : WebPage
{
    public ApiResponse EnableEditing()
    {
        // 1. Instantiate the response.
        ApiResponse _ApiResponse = new ApiResponse();

        // 2. Remove the "readonly" attribute from the input fields to make them editable.
        _ApiResponse.RemoveElementAttribute("username", HtmlAttributes.readonly);
        _ApiResponse.RemoveElementAttribute("email", HtmlAttributes.readonly);

        // 3. Hide the "Edit" button and show the "Save" button.
        _ApiResponse.SetElementStyle("editBtn", "display", "none");
        _ApiResponse.SetElementStyle("saveBtn", "display", "inline-block");

        // 4. Return the response.
        return _ApiResponse;
    }

    // Placeholder for the SaveChanges function
    public ApiResponse SaveChanges()
    {
        return new ApiResponse();
    }
}

RemoveElementAttributeByName Method

  The ApiResponse.RemoveElementAttributeByName method is a command used to dynamically remove a specific HTML attribute from all elements on the client's page that share the same name attribute. This method provides a powerful and efficient way to perform bulk updates on a group of related elements in a single operation, without requiring a page refresh. It is the direct counterpart to SetElementAttributeByName and is ideal for scenarios where a group state needs to be reverted. Common use cases include deselecting all checkboxes in a group by removing the checked attribute or re-enabling a set of related input fields by removing their disabled attribute.

Syntax:
VB
Public Sub RemoveElementAttributeByName(elementName As String, attribute As String)
C#
public void RemoveElementAttributeByName(string elementName, string attribute)
Parameters:
  • elementName (String): Required. The value of the name attribute shared by the group of HTML elements from which the attribute will be removed.
  • attribute (String): Required. The name of the HTML attribute to remove from each element in the group (e.g., "checked", "disabled", "readonly"). It is recommended to use the constants from the HtmlAttributes structure.
Usage and Example:

This method is highly effective for managing form element groups. A clear example is providing a "Clear Selection" button for a list of checkboxes.

Client-Side HTML (or generated by OnInitialized):
<p>Select your interests:</p>
<input type="checkbox" name="interest" value="tech" checked="checked"> Technology<br>
<input type="checkbox" name="interest" value="sports" checked="checked"> Sports<br>
<input type="checkbox" name="interest" value="music" checked="checked"> Music<br>
<br>
<button onclick="ClearInterests()">Clear Selection</button>
JavaScript (myPage.js):
function ClearInterests() {
    // Call the server function to clear the checkboxes.
    // No data needs to be sent.
    $ApiRequest();
}
VB.NET Server-Side Example (myPage.vb)
Imports skylite

Public Class myPage
    Inherits WebPage

    Public Function ClearInterests() As ApiResponse
        ' 1. Instantiate the response.
        Dim _ApiResponse As New ApiResponse()

        ' 2. Target all elements with name="interest" and remove their "checked" attribute.
        '    This will visually uncheck all the boxes in the group.
        _ApiResponse.RemoveElementAttributeByName("interest", HtmlAttributes.checked)

        ' 3. Return the response.
        Return _ApiResponse
    End Function

End Class
C# Server-Side Example (myPage.cs)
using skylite;

public class myPage : WebPage
{
    public ApiResponse ClearInterests()
    {
        // 1. Instantiate the response.
        ApiResponse _ApiResponse = new ApiResponse();

        // 2. Target all elements with name="interest" and remove their "checked" attribute.
        //    This will visually uncheck all the boxes in the group.
        _ApiResponse.RemoveElementAttributeByName("interest", HtmlAttributes.checked);

        // 3. Return the response.
        return _ApiResponse;
    }
}

SetElementValue Method

  The ApiResponse.SetElementValue method is a command specifically designed to dynamically set the value property of form input elements on the client's page. This action is performed in real-time without a page refresh and targets an element by its unique id. There is a crucial distinction between this method and SetElementContents: SetElementValue modifies the value attribute/property, which is used for elements like "input", "textarea", "select", and "button". In contrast, SetElementContents modifies the innerHTML, which is used for container elements like "div" or "span". This method is essential for programmatically populating or resetting form fields from the server.

Syntax:
VB
Public Sub SetElementValue(elementId As String, value As String)
C#
public void SetElementValue(string elementId, string value)
Parameters:
  • elementId (String): Required. The unique id of the form element whose value you want to set.
  • value (String): Required. The new value to assign to the element's value property. To clear the value of an input field, you would pass an empty string ("").
Usage and Example:

This method is commonly used to update form data based on a user's action or a server-side calculation. A simple and effective example is providing a "Reset" or "Clear" button for a specific input field.

Client-Side HTML (or generated by OnInitialized):
<input type="text" id="userComment" placeholder="Enter your comment...">
<button onclick="ClearComment()">Clear</button>
JavaScript (myPage.js):
function ClearComment() {
    // This function calls the server-side function of the same name.
    // No data payload is necessary.
    $ApiRequest();
}
VB.NET Server-Side Example (myPage.vb)
Imports skylite

Public Class myPage
    Inherits WebPage

    Public Function ClearComment() As ApiResponse
        ' 1. Instantiate the response.
        Dim _ApiResponse As New ApiResponse()

        ' 2. Target the element with id="userComment" and set its value to an empty string.
        '    This will clear any text the user has typed into the input field.
        _ApiResponse.SetElementValue("userComment", "")

        ' 3. Return the response.
        Return _ApiResponse
    End Function

End Class

    {

        // 2. Target the element with id="userComment" and set its value to an empty string.
        //    This will clear any text the user has typed into the input field.
        _ApiResponse.SetElementValue("userComment", "");

        // 3. Return the response.
        return _ApiResponse;
    }
}

SetElementValueByName Method

  The ApiResponse.SetElementValueByName method is a command that dynamically sets the value property for all elements on the client's page that share a common name attribute. This method provides a convenient way to perform a bulk update on the values of related form elements, such as a group of text inputs or hidden fields, in a single operation without a page refresh. It is particularly useful in scenarios where a single server-side action needs to reset or populate multiple related fields to a uniform value.

Syntax:
VB
Public Sub SetElementValueByName(elementName As String, value As String)
C#
public void SetElementValueByName(string elementName, string value)
Parameters:
  • elementName (String): Required. The value of the name attribute shared by the group of form elements whose value you want to set.
  • value (String): Required. The new value to assign to each element's value property. An empty string ("") can be used to clear the value of all matched elements.
Usage and Example:

This method is effective for managing groups of inputs. For example, in a form with multiple "quantity" fields for different products, a button could reset all quantities to zero.

Client-Side HTML (or generated by OnInitialized):
<p>Product A Quantity: <input type="number" id="qty_a" name="quantity" value="5"></p>
<p>Product B Quantity: <input type="number" id="qty_b" name="quantity" value="12"></p>
<p>Product C Quantity: <input type="number" id="qty_c" name="quantity" value="3"></p>
<br>
<button onclick="ResetQuantities()">Reset All to 0</button>
JavaScript (myPage.js):

}

SetElementContents Method

  The ApiResponse.SetElementContents method is a cornerstone command for creating dynamic, single-page-like experiences within the SkyLite framework. Its primary function is to completely replace the inner HTML content of a specific container element on the client's page, targeting the element by its unique id. This is the ideal method for updating non-input elements like "div", "span", "p", or "td". It allows for partial page updates, where a section of the page can be refreshed with new, server-generated content without reloading the entire document. This is fundamentally different from SetElementValue, which is used to change the value property of form inputs.

Syntax:
VB
' VB.NET Overload 1: Using a String
Public Sub SetElementContents(elementId As String, contentsHtml As String)

' VB.NET Overload 2: Using an HtmlDocument
Public Sub SetElementContents(elementId As String, HtmlDoc As HtmlDocument)
C#
// C# Overload 1: Using a String
public void SetElementContents(string elementId, string contentsHtml)

// C# Overload 2: Using an HtmlDocument
public void SetElementContents(string elementId, HtmlDocument HtmlDoc)
Parameters:
  • elementId (String): Required. The unique id of the HTML container element whose content will be replaced.
  • contentsHtml (String): Required. A string of well-formed HTML that will become the new innerHTML of the target element.
  • HtmlDoc (HtmlDocument): Required. A SkyLite HtmlDocument object. The framework uses the HtmlBodyText property of this object as the new content. This is useful when the new content is complex and built using SkyLite UI controls.
Usage and Example:

A perfect use case is loading detailed information into a specific panel when a user clicks an item in a list.

Client-Side HTML (or generated by OnInitialized):
<p>Select a product:</p>
<ul>
    <li onclick="LoadProductDetails('P123')" style="cursor:pointer;">Product A</li>
    <li onclick="LoadProductDetails('P456')" style="cursor:pointer;">Product B</li>
</ul>

<div id="productDetailsContainer" style="border:1px solid #ccc; padding:10px; margin-top:10px;">
    <!-- Product details will be loaded here -->
</div>
JavaScript (myPage.js):
function LoadProductDetails(productId) {
    var data = [
        { key: 'prodId', vlu: productId }
    ];
    // Explicitly call the server function of the same name
    $ApiRequest(this, JSON.stringify(data));
}
VB.NET Server-Side Example (myPage.vb)
Imports skylite
Imports skylite.ToolKit

Public Class myPage
    Inherits WebPage

    Public Function LoadProductDetails() As ApiResponse
        Dim productId As String = GetDataValue("prodId")
        Dim productDetailsHtml As String = ""

        ' --- Logic to fetch product details from a database based on productId ---
        Select Case productId
            Case "P123"
                productDetailsHtml = "<h3>Product A Details</h3><p>Description for Product A.</p>"
            Case "P456"
                productDetailsHtml = "<h3>Product B Details</h3><p>Description for Product B.</p>"
            Case Else
                productDetailsHtml = "<p>Product not found.</p>"
        End Select

        ' 1. Instantiate the response.
        Dim _ApiResponse As New ApiResponse()

        ' 2. Target the element with id="productDetailsContainer" and set its inner content.
        _ApiResponse.SetElementContents("productDetailsContainer", productDetailsHtml)

        ' 3. Return the response.
        Return _ApiResponse
    End Function

End Class
C# Server-Side Example (myPage.cs)
using skylite;
using skylite.ToolKit;

public class myPage : WebPage
{
    public ApiResponse LoadProductDetails()
    {
        string productId = GetDataValue("prodId");
        string productDetailsHtml = "";

        // --- Logic to fetch product details from a database based on productId ---
        switch (productId)
        {
            case "P123":
                productDetailsHtml = "<h3>Product A Details</h3><p>Description for Product A.</p>";
                break;
            case "P456":
                productDetailsHtml = "<h3>Product B Details</h3><p>Description for Product B.</p>";
                break;
            default:
                productDetailsHtml = "<p>Product not found.</p>";
                break;
        }

        // 1. Instantiate the response.
        ApiResponse _ApiResponse = new ApiResponse();

        // 2. Target the element with id="productDetailsContainer" and set its inner content.
        _ApiResponse.SetElementContents("productDetailsContainer", productDetailsHtml);

        // 3. Return the response.
        return _ApiResponse;
    }
}

SetElementContentsByName Method

  The ApiResponse.SetElementContentsByName method is a powerful command for performing bulk content updates on the client's page. Unlike its counterpart SetElementContents which targets a single element by its unique id, this method updates the inner HTML content of all elements that share a common name attribute. This provides an efficient way to modify multiple sections of a page simultaneously with a single server command, which is ideal for user interfaces with repeating structures or status indicators. This method is particularly useful for updating multiple status panels, resetting several information boxes, or populating identically structured containers across a UI.

Syntax:
VB
Public Sub SetElementContentsByName(elementName As String, contentsHtml As String)
C#
public void SetElementContentsByName(string elementName, string contentsHtml)
Parameters:
  • elementName (String): Required. The value of the name attribute shared by the group of HTML container elements whose content will be replaced.
  • contentsHtml (String): Required. A string of well-formed HTML that will become the new innerHTML for every matched element.
Usage and Example:

This method is highly effective in situations where the same information needs to be displayed in multiple places. Imagine a simple quiz where clicking a "Show Answers" button reveals the correct answer below each question.

Client-Side HTML (or generated by OnInitialized):
<div class="question-block">
    <p>1. What is the capital of France?</p>
    <div name="answer-reveal" style="color:green; font-weight:bold;"></div>
</div>

<div class="question-block">
    <p>2. What is 2 + 2?</p>
    <div name="answer-reveal" style="color:green; font-weight:bold;"></div>
</div>

<button onclick="ShowAnswers()">Show All Answers</button>
JavaScript (myPage.js):
function ShowAnswers() {
    // Call the server function of the same name. No data is needed.
    $ApiRequest();
}

    Inherits WebPage

        ' 1. Instantiate the response.
        Dim _ApiResponse As New ApiResponse()

        ' 2. Target all elements with name="answer-reveal" and set their content.
        '    This single command will update both answer divs on the page.
        _ApiResponse.SetElementContentsByName("answer-reveal", answerHtml)

        ' 3. Return the response.
        Return _ApiResponse
    End Function

End Class

        // 2. Target all elements with name="answer-reveal" and set their content.
        //    This single command will update both answer divs on the page.

        // 3. Return the response.
        return _ApiResponse;
    }

AddElementContents Method

  The ApiResponse.AddElementContents method is a command used to dynamically append HTML content to the end of a specific container element's existing content on the client's page. It identifies the target element by its unique id. Unlike SetElementContents which replaces the entire inner HTML, AddElementContents preserves the current content and adds the new content after it. This makes it the ideal tool for incrementally adding items to a list, logging real-time status updates to a console-like "div", or adding new comments to a discussion thread without refreshing the entire page.

Syntax:
VB
' VB.NET Overload 1: Using a String
Public Sub AddElementContents(elementId As String, contentsHtml As String)

' VB.NET Overload 2: Using an HtmlDocument
Public Sub AddElementContents(elementId As String, HtmlDoc As HtmlDocument)
C#
// C# Overload 1: Using a String
public void AddElementContents(string elementId, string contentsHtml)

// C# Overload 2: Using an HtmlDocument
public void AddElementContents(string elementId, HtmlDocument HtmlDoc)
Parameters:
  • elementId (String): Required. The unique id of the HTML container element to which the new content will be appended.
  • contentsHtml (String): Required. A string of well-formed HTML that will be added to the end of the target element's innerHTML.
  • HtmlDoc (HtmlDocument): Required. A SkyLite HtmlDocument object. The framework uses the HtmlBodyText property of this object as the content to be appended.
Usage and Example:

A common use case for this method is adding new entries to a list or log. Imagine a simple "To-Do" application where a user can add new tasks to a list.

Client-Side HTML (or generated by OnInitialized):
<ul id="todoList">
    <li>Learn SkyLite Framework</li>
</ul>
<input type="text" id="newTask" placeholder="Add a new task...">
<button onclick="AddTask()">Add Task</button>
JavaScript (myPage.js):
function AddTask() {
    var taskText = document.getElementById('newTask').value;
    if (taskText.trim() === '') return; // Don't add empty tasks

    var data = [
        { key: 'task', vlu: taskText }
    ];
    $ApiRequest(this, JSON.stringify(data));
    
    // Clear the input field for the next task
    document.getElementById('newTask').value = '';
}
VB.NET Server-Side Example (myPage.vb)
Imports skylite
Imports skylite.ToolKit

Public Class myPage
    Inherits WebPage

    Public Function AddTask() As ApiResponse
        ' Get the new task text from the client.
        Dim newTaskText As String = GetDataValue("task")

        ' Create the new list item as an HTML string.
        Dim newListItemHtml As String = "<li>" & newTaskText & "</li>"

        ' 1. Instantiate the response.
        Dim _ApiResponse As New ApiResponse()

        ' 2. Target the element with id="todoList" and append the new <li> element to it.
        _ApiResponse.AddElementContents("todoList", newListItemHtml)

        ' 3. Return the response.
        Return _ApiResponse
    End Function

End Class
C# Server-Side Example (myPage.cs)
using skylite;
using skylite.ToolKit;

public class myPage : WebPage
{
    public ApiResponse AddTask()
    {
        // Get the new task text from the client.
        string newTaskText = GetDataValue("task");

        // Create the new list item as an HTML string.
        string newListItemHtml = "<li>" + newTaskText + "</li>";

        // 1. Instantiate the response.
        ApiResponse _ApiResponse = new ApiResponse();

        // 2. Target the element with id="todoList" and append the new <li> element to it.
        _ApiResponse.AddElementContents("todoList", newListItemHtml);

        // 3. Return the response.
        return _ApiResponse;
    }
}

AddElementContentsByName Method

  The ApiResponse.AddElementContentsByName method is a command that dynamically appends HTML content to the end of the existing content of all elements that share a common name attribute. This method is the "append" counterpart to SetElementContentsByName and is used for performing bulk additions to multiple container elements simultaneously without a page refresh. It is particularly useful for interfaces where multiple, identically named sections need to be updated incrementally. For example, you could add a new log entry to several log displays at once, or append a new comment to both a main thread and a "latest comments" sidebar simultaneously.

Syntax:
VB
Public Sub AddElementContentsByName(elementName As String, contentsHtml As String)
C#
public void AddElementContentsByName(string elementName, string contentsHtml)
Parameters:
  • elementName (String): Required. The value of the name attribute shared by the group of HTML container elements to which the new content will be appended.
  • contentsHtml (String): Required. A string of well-formed HTML that will be added to the end of the innerHTML of every matched element.
Usage and Example:

This method is effective for synchronizing content additions across different parts of a UI. Imagine a dashboard with two panels: a main activity feed and a smaller "summary" feed, both of which need to show the latest system event.

Client-Side HTML (or generated by OnInitialized):
<h4>Main Activity Feed</h4>
<div id="mainFeed" name="activity-feed" style="border:1px solid #ccc; padding:5px;">
    <div>Initial event logged.</div>
</div>

<h4>Summary Feed</h4>
<div id="summaryFeed" name="activity-feed" style="border:1px solid #ccc; padding:5px;">
    <div>Initial event logged.</div>
</div>

<button onclick="LogNewEvent()">Log New System Event</button>

}

RemoveElementContents Method

  The ApiResponse.RemoveElementContents method is a command used to dynamically clear all inner HTML content from a specific container element on the client's page. It identifies the target element by its unique id. This action effectively empties the element, leaving the element itself intact on the page but with no content inside it. This method is the logical counterpart to SetElementContents and AddElementContents and is essential for tasks such as clearing a results panel before displaying new search results, resetting a status log, or emptying a preview container.

Syntax:

Public Sub RemoveElementContents(elementId As String)

public void RemoveElementContents(string elementId)

It dynamically removes all inner HTML content from every element that shares a specified name attribute. This action leaves the container elements themselves on the page but empties them of their content. It is the direct counterpart to SetElementContentsByName and AddElementContentsByName and is highly efficient for resetting multiple, related sections of a user interface with a single command. Common use cases include clearing all error message containers in a form, resetting multiple preview panes, or emptying all log displays on a dashboard.
